========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor... I came because... I just don't feel right anymore. Even when my children come over and sit with me on the porch, or when the sun is shining so beautifully on my garden—oh, you should see my hydrangeas this year, they finally bloomed after I moved them to the east side of the house. I always told my late husband they needed morning sun, but he insisted on planting them by the oak tree. He was stubborn like that, bless him. He bought that oak tree as a sapling back in '78... 

But anyway, what I mean is, even when I'm looking at those flowers or sitting with my kids, I just feel completely flat inside. It's like the part of me that used to feel glad or... or comforted by those things just completely shut off. I don't know where the feeling went.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, it's been... I'd say about two weeks now. Yes, just about two weeks. It started right after my husband collapsed. He was right there in the kitchen, and I... I was making a pot roast. You know, the butcher down at the corner market—well, his son runs it mostly now, but he always saves the best chuck roasts for me because I've been shopping there since 1985. The prices have gone up terribly, of course. I nearly fainted when I saw what they charge for a dozen eggs lately, it's just absolute robbery... 

But anyway, I was standing at the stove, and Arthur just went down. And then the paramedics came, and they were working on him right there on my kitchen floor. They had these big heavy boots on, and I remember thinking about how they tracked in so much dirt on my nice linoleum... they worked on him right between the island and the sink. So yes, ever since that happened two weeks ago, I've just been feeling this way.

Well, like I was just saying about the garden... no. Not at all. I try, I really do. I used to love knitting, too, besides the gardening. It's like the switch for feeling glad about anything has just been completely turned off.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, like I was saying, it just... it feels completely flat. Even with my children. They try to be so sweet, coming over and checking on me. My daughter Susan brought over a casserole just yesterday. She makes it with those little crispy fried onions on top, you know the ones that come in the can? I used to make it for Arthur every Thanksgiving. He wouldn't eat the green beans unless they were smothered in cream of mushroom soup and those onions. Thanksgiving was always such a big production in our house. We'd have twenty people sometimes, back when Arthur's brothers were still alive. We'd have to set up two card tables in the living room just to fit everyone, and the noise... oh, the noise was something else. 

But... yes, to answer your question, when Susan brought that casserole over and sat with me, I just... I didn't feel anything. I knew I should feel grateful, or happy to see her, but it was just... nothing. Just flat.

Weight? Oh, I don't really know. I haven't stepped on a scale in... well, it's been ages. I used to have one of those nice mechanical ones, the kind with the little dial that spins around. Arthur bought it for me back in, oh, it must have been 1998. We got it at Sears. Do you remember when Sears used to be the place to go for everything? We bought our first refrigerator there, too. A big avocado green thing. It matched the stove perfectly. That was when everyone was doing their kitchens in avocado green or harvest gold. We had this terrible floral wallpaper to go with it... I finally made Arthur help me steam it off about ten years later, and what a mess that was. Little pieces of paper stuck to our shoes for weeks... 

But, oh, my weight. No, I haven't noticed any change. I just haven't been paying attention to it.

Guilty? No, I... I don't think so. I mean, it happened so fast. One minute he was just sitting there in his favorite recliner—the brown leather one, you know? We got that chair at a discount furniture place out on Route 9. The man there, I think his name was Sal, he gave us such a good deal because there was a tiny scratch on the back leg. You couldn't even see it unless you got down on your hands and knees. Arthur loved that chair. He'd sit there every evening watching Jeopardy, shouting out the answers. He was always so good at history questions, but he couldn't get the pop culture ones to save his life. He didn't know who Madonna was until the late nineties... 

But, no, doctor. I don't feel guilty. I just... I don't feel much of anything at all.

It started right after my husband collapsed. He was right there in the kitchen, and I... I was making a pot roast. You know, the butcher down at the corner market—well, his son runs it mostly now, but he always saves the best chuck roasts for me because I've been shopping there since 1985. The prices have gone up terribly, of course. I nearly fainted when I saw what they charge for a dozen eggs lately, it's just absolute robbery... \n\nBut anyway, I was standing at the stove, and Arthur just went down. And then the paramedics came, and they were working on him right there on my kitchen floor. They had these big heavy boots on, and I remember thinking about how they tracked in so much dirt on my nice linoleum... they worked on him right between the island and the sink. So yes, ever since that happened two weeks ago, I've just been feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to enjoy activities or hobbies that you used to find pleasurable before these past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying about the garden... no. Not at all. I try, I really do. I used to love knitting, too, besides the gardening. I was right in the middle of making a nice blue cardigan for my grandson, David. He's at the state university now, studying civil engineering. Such a smart boy. His father—my son, Richard—really wanted him to go into business, but David has always been so good with his hands. He used to take apart my toaster when he was just a little thing, leaving springs and screws all over the kitchen table... I was always finding pieces of it for months. \n\nBut, oh, what were you asking... right, the hobbies. No, doctor. I pick up those knitting needles now, or I go sit out by those hydrangeas, and it's exactly like I was just telling you. I just feel completely flat inside. That warm, happy feeling I used to get when I finished a row of stitches or when the kids come to visit... it's just gone. It's like the switch for feeling glad about anything has just been completely turned off.\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel a sense of connection or pleasure when spending time with your children, or does that also feel flat to you?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, it just... it feels completely flat. Even with my children. They try to be so sweet, coming over and checking on me. My daughter Susan brought over a casserole just yesterday. She makes it with those little crispy fried onions on top, you know the ones that come in the can? I used to make it for Arthur every Thanksgiving. He wouldn't eat the green beans unless they were smothered in cream of mushroom soup and those onions. Thanksgiving was always such a big production in our house. We'd have twenty people sometimes, back when Arthur's brothers were still alive. We'd have to set up two card tables in the living room just to fit everyone, and the noise... oh, the noise was something else. \n\nBut... yes, to answer your question, when Susan brought that casserole over and sat with me, I just... I didn't feel anything. I knew I should feel grateful, or happy to see her, but it was just... nothing. Just flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Weight? Oh, I don't really know. I haven't stepped on a scale in... well, it's been ages. I used to have one of those nice mechanical ones, the kind with the little dial that spins around. Arthur bought it for me back in, oh, it must have been 1998. We got it at Sears. Do you remember when Sears used to be the place to go for everything? We bought our first refrigerator there, too. A big avocado green thing. It matched the stove perfectly. That was when everyone was doing their kitchens in avocado green or harvest gold. We had this terrible floral wallpaper to go with it... I finally made Arthur help me steam it off about ten years later, and what a mess that was. Little pieces of paper stuck to our shoes for weeks... \n\nBut, oh, my weight. No, I haven't noticed any change. I just haven't been paying attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your husband's collapse or anything else?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I... I don't think so. I mean, it happened so fast. One minute he was just sitting there in his favorite recliner—the brown leather one, you know? We got that chair at a discount furniture place out on Route 9. The man there, I think his name was Sal, he gave us such a good deal because there was a tiny scratch on the back leg. You couldn't even see it unless you got down on your hands and knees. Arthur loved that chair. He'd sit there every evening watching Jeopardy, shouting out the answers. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Pleasure? No... no, I don't think so. I was just looking out the window this morning, at my garden. The sun was hitting the hydrangeas just right. Arthur and I planted those hydrangeas when we first moved into the house. Oh, they must be forty years old now. We had to dig up this awful rocky soil to get them in, and Arthur broke his favorite trowel doing it. He was so mad about that trowel, he tried to fix it with duct tape, but it never held. He was always trying to fix things with duct tape. He taped up the handle on the lawnmower, the hinge on the screen door... he even tried to fix a leaky pipe under the sink once. Let me tell you, that was a disaster. We had water all over the linoleum, and we had to call a plumber anyway. The plumber, a nice young man named Ricky, charged us an arm and a leg, but he said Arthur's tape job was the funniest thing he'd ever seen...

But the garden... yes. The sun was on it, and it looked so pretty, but I just... I felt flat inside. Even when the kids sit with me, like Susan yesterday, it's the same. It's like the part of me that could feel glad about the flowers, or comforted by my children... it just shut off.

That warm, happy feeling I used to get when I finished a row of stitches or when the kids come to visit... it's just gone. It's like the switch for feeling glad about anything has just been completely turned off.\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel a sense of connection or pleasure when spending time with your children, or does that also feel flat to you?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, it just... it feels completely flat. Even with my children. They try to be so sweet, coming over and checking on me. My daughter Susan brought over a casserole just yesterday. She makes it with those little crispy fried onions on top, you know the ones that come in the can? I used to make it for Arthur every Thanksgiving. He wouldn't eat the green beans unless they were smothered in cream of mushroom soup and those onions. Thanksgiving was always such a big production in our house. We'd have twenty people sometimes, back when Arthur's brothers were still alive. We'd have to set up two card tables in the living room just to fit everyone, and the noise... oh, the noise was something else. \n\nBut... yes, to answer your question, when Susan brought that casserole over and sat with me, I just... I didn't feel anything. I knew I should feel grateful, or happy to see her, but it was just... nothing. Just flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Weight? Oh, I don't really know. I haven't stepped on a scale in... well, it's been ages. I used to have one of those nice mechanical ones, the kind with the little dial that spins around. Arthur bought it for me back in, oh, it must have been 1998. We got it at Sears. Do you remember when Sears used to be the place to go for everything? We bought our first refrigerator there, too. A big avocado green thing. It matched the stove perfectly. That was when everyone was doing their kitchens in avocado green or harvest gold. We had this terrible floral wallpaper to go with it... I finally made Arthur help me steam it off about ten years later, and what a mess that was. Little pieces of paper stuck to our shoes for weeks... \n\nBut, oh, my weight. No, I haven't noticed any change. I just haven't been paying attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your husband's collapse or anything else?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I... I don't think so. I mean, it happened so fast. One minute he was just sitting there in his favorite recliner—the brown leather one, you know? We got that chair at a discount furniture place out on Route 9. The man there, I think his name was Sal, he gave us such a good deal because there was a tiny scratch on the back leg. You couldn't even see it unless you got down on your hands and knees. Arthur loved that chair. He'd sit there every evening watching Jeopardy, shouting out the answers. He was always so good at history questions, but he couldn't get the pop culture ones to save his life. He didn't know who Madonna was until the late nineties... \n\nBut, no, doctor. I don't feel guilty. I just... I don't feel much of anything at all.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No... no, I don't think so. I was just looking out the window this morning, at my garden. The sun was hitting the hydrangeas just right. Arthur and I planted those hydrangeas when we first moved into the house. Oh, they must be forty years old now. We had to dig up this awful rocky soil to get them in, and Arthur broke his favorite trowel doing it. He was so mad about that trowel, he tried to fix it with duct tape, but it never held. He was always trying to fix things with duct tape. He taped up the handle on the lawnmower, the hinge on the screen door... he even tried to fix a leaky pipe under the sink once. Let me tell you, that was a disaster. We had water all over the linoleum, and we had to call a plumber anyway. The plumber, a nice young man named Ricky, charged us an arm and a leg, but he said Arthur's tape job was the funniest thing he'd ever seen...\n\nBut the garden... yes. The sun was on it, and it looked so pretty, but I just... I felt flat inside. Even when the kids sit with me, like Susan yesterday, it's the same. Hurting myself? Oh, heavens, no. I haven't thought about anything like that. I wouldn't even know how to... well, I suppose people do all sorts of things. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable—she lived two houses down, the one with the big oak tree in the front yard that drops acorns all over the sidewalk every fall. Arthur used to spend hours sweeping those acorns up. He said if he didn't, someone would slip and break their neck, and then we'd be sued. He was always worrying about being sued. He bought this special push broom just for the acorns, with these stiff red bristles. He got it at the hardware store downtown, the one that closed when the big home improvement place opened out by the highway. It was a shame, really. The man who owned it, Mr. Henderson, he always gave the neighborhood kids a free piece of hard candy from a glass jar on the counter. Peppermint, usually, or butterscotch. Arthur loved butterscotch. He kept a roll of those little yellow candies in the glove compartment of the car, and they'd get all melted together in the summer...

But no, doctor. No thoughts like that. I'm just sitting here.

They had these big heavy boots on, and I remember thinking about how they tracked in so much dirt on my nice linoleum... they worked on him right between the island and the sink. So yes, ever since that happened two weeks ago, I've just been feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to enjoy activities or hobbies that you used to find pleasurable before these past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying about the garden... no. Not at all. I try, I really do. I used to love knitting, too, besides the gardening. I was right in the middle of making a nice blue cardigan for my grandson, David. He's at the state university now, studying civil engineering. Such a smart boy. His father—my son, Richard—really wanted him to go into business, but David has always been so good with his hands. He used to take apart my toaster when he was just a little thing, leaving springs and screws all over the kitchen table... I was always finding pieces of it for months. \n\nBut, oh, what were you asking... right, the hobbies. No, doctor. I pick up those knitting needles now, or I go sit out by those hydrangeas, and it's exactly like I was just telling you. I just feel completely flat inside. That warm, happy feeling I used to get when I finished a row of stitches or when the kids come to visit... it's just gone. It's like the switch for feeling glad about anything has just been completely turned off.\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel a sense of connection or pleasure when spending time with your children, or does that also feel flat to you?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, it just... it feels completely flat. Even with my children. They try to be so sweet, coming over and checking on me. My daughter Susan brought over a casserole just yesterday. She makes it with those little crispy fried onions on top, you know the ones that come in the can? I used to make it for Arthur every Thanksgiving. He wouldn't eat the green beans unless they were smothered in cream of mushroom soup and those onions. Thanksgiving was always such a big production in our house. We'd have twenty people sometimes, back when Arthur's brothers were still alive. We'd have to set up two card tables in the living room just to fit everyone, and the noise... oh, the noise was something else. \n\nBut... yes, to answer your question, when Susan brought that casserole over and sat with me, I just... I didn't feel anything. I knew I should feel grateful, or happy to see her, but it was just... nothing. Just flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Weight? Oh, I don't really know. I haven't stepped on a scale in... well, it's been ages. I used to have one of those nice mechanical ones, the kind with the little dial that spins around. Arthur bought it for me back in, oh, it must have been 1998. We got it at Sears. Do you remember when Sears used to be the place to go for everything? We bought our first refrigerator there, too. A big avocado green thing. It matched the stove perfectly. That was when everyone was doing their kitchens in avocado green or harvest gold. We had this terrible floral wallpaper to go with it... I finally made Arthur help me steam it off about ten years later, and what a mess that was. Little pieces of paper stuck to our shoes for weeks... \n\nBut, oh, my weight. No, I haven't noticed any change. I just haven't been paying attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your husband's collapse or anything else?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I... I don't think so. I mean, it happened so fast. One minute he was just sitting there in his favorite recliner—the brown leather one, you know? We got that chair at a discount furniture place out on Route 9. The man there, I think his name was Sal, he gave us such a good deal because there was a tiny scratch on the back leg. You couldn't even see it unless you got down on your hands and knees. Arthur loved that chair. He'd sit there every evening watching Jeopardy, shouting out the answers. He was always so good at history questions, but he couldn't get the pop culture ones to save his life. He didn't know who Madonna was until the late nineties... \n\nBut, no, doctor. I don't feel guilty. I just... I don't feel much of anything at all.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No... no, I don't think so. I was just looking out the window this morning, at my garden. The sun was hitting the hydrangeas just right. Arthur and I planted those hydrangeas when we first moved into the house. Oh, they must be forty years old now. We had to dig up this awful rocky soil to get them in, and Arthur broke his favorite trowel doing it. He was so mad about that trowel, he tried to fix it with duct tape, but it never held. He was always trying to fix things with duct tape. He taped up the handle on the lawnmower, the hinge on the screen door... he even tried to fix a leaky pipe under the sink once. Let me tell you, that was a disaster. We had water all over the linoleum, and we had to call a plumber anyway. The plumber, a nice young man named Ricky, charged us an arm and a leg, but he said Arthur's tape job was the funniest thing he'd ever seen...\n\nBut the garden... yes. The sun was on it, and it looked so pretty, but I just... I felt flat inside. Even when the kids sit with me, like Susan yesterday, it's the same. It's like the part of me that could feel glad about the flowers, or comforted by my children... it just shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself since your husband's collapse?\n\nPatient: Hurting myself? Oh, heavens, no. I haven't thought about anything like that. I wouldn't even know how to... well, I suppose people do all sorts of things. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable—she lived two houses down, the one with the big oak tree in the front yard that drops acorns all over the sidewalk every fall. Arthur used to spend hours sweeping those acorns up. He said if he didn't, someone would slip and break their neck, and then we'd be sued. He was always worrying about being sued. He bought this special push broom just for the acorns, with these stiff red bristles. He got it at the hardware store downtown, the one that closed when the big home improvement place opened out by the highway. It was a shame, really. The man who owned it, Mr. Henderson, he always gave the neighborhood kids a free piece of hard candy from a glass jar on the counter. Peppermint, usually, or butterscotch. Arthur loved butterscotch. He kept a roll of those little yellow candies in the glove compartment of the car, and they'd get all melted together in the summer...\n\nBut no, doctor. No thoughts like that. I'm just sitting here.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Pleasure? No, I... I don't think I can. I mean, I was just saying about the garden, wasn't I? The sun was shining on it, and it was so pretty, but I just felt... flat. Empty, really. And it's the same with my children. Susan came over yesterday. She brought over a casserole, one of those tuna noodle ones with the crushed potato chips on top. I used to make those for the kids when they were little. Arthur hated it, though. He always said tuna should be in a sandwich with mayonnaise and a piece of crisp lettuce, not baked in an oven. He was very particular about his sandwiches. He'd only eat that one brand of bread, the kind in the yellow wrapper with the red letters. If I bought the store brand, he'd know right away. He said the texture was all wrong, like eating a sponge. He could be so stubborn about food. Once, we went to this Italian restaurant for our anniversary, and he sent his spaghetti back three times because the sauce wasn't hot enough... 

But, yes... even when Susan is sitting right there next to me, or when I'm looking at the flowers, it's just... nothing. It's like that part of me, the part that used to feel glad or comforted, it just completely shut off.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor... I came because... I just don't feel right anymore. Even when my children come over and sit with me on the porch, or when the sun is shining so beautifully on my garden—oh, you should see my hydrangeas this year, they finally bloomed after I moved them to the east side of the house. I always told my late husband they needed morning sun, but he insisted on planting them by the oak tree. He was stubborn like that, bless him. He bought that oak tree as a sapling back in '78... \n\nBut anyway, what I mean is, even when I'm looking at those flowers or sitting with my kids, I just feel completely flat inside. It's like the part of me that used to feel glad or... or comforted by those things just completely shut off. I don't know where the feeling went.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's been... I'd say about two weeks now. Yes, just about two weeks. It started right after my husband collapsed. He was right there in the kitchen, and I... I was making a pot roast. You know, the butcher down at the corner market—well, his son runs it mostly now, but he always saves the best chuck roasts for me because I've been shopping there since 1985. The prices have gone up terribly, of course. I nearly fainted when I saw what they charge for a dozen eggs lately, it's just absolute robbery... \n\nBut anyway, I was standing at the stove, and Arthur just went down. And then the paramedics came, and they were working on him right there on my kitchen floor. They had these big heavy boots on, and I remember thinking about how they tracked in so much dirt on my nice linoleum... they worked on him right between the island and the sink. So yes, ever since that happened two weeks ago, I've just been feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to enjoy activities or hobbies that you used to find pleasurable before these past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying about the garden... no. Not at all. I try, I really do. I used to love knitting, too, besides the gardening. I was right in the middle of making a nice blue cardigan for my grandson, David. He's at the state university now, studying civil engineering. Such a smart boy. His father—my son, Richard—really wanted him to go into business, but David has always been so good with his hands. He used to take apart my toaster when he was just a little thing, leaving springs and screws all over the kitchen table... I was always finding pieces of it for months. \n\nBut, oh, what were you asking... right, the hobbies. No, doctor. I pick up those knitting needles now, or I go sit out by those hydrangeas, and it's exactly like I was just telling you. I just feel completely flat inside. That warm, happy feeling I used to get when I finished a row of stitches or when the kids come to visit... it's just gone. It's like the switch for feeling glad about anything has just been completely turned off.\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel a sense of connection or pleasure when spending time with your children, or does that also feel flat to you?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, it just... it feels completely flat. Even with my children. They try to be so sweet, coming over and checking on me. My daughter Susan brought over a casserole just yesterday. She makes it with those little crispy fried onions on top, you know the ones that come in the can? I used to make it for Arthur every Thanksgiving. He wouldn't eat the green beans unless they were smothered in cream of mushroom soup and those onions. Thanksgiving was always such a big production in our house. We'd have twenty people sometimes, back when Arthur's brothers were still alive. We'd have to set up two card tables in the living room just to fit everyone, and the noise... oh, the noise was something else. \n\nBut... yes, to answer your question, when Susan brought that casserole over and sat with me, I just... I didn't feel anything. I knew I should feel grateful, or happy to see her, but it was just... nothing. Just flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Weight? Oh, I don't really know. I haven't stepped on a scale in... well, it's been ages. I used to have one of those nice mechanical ones, the kind with the little dial that spins around. Arthur bought it for me back in, oh, it must have been 1998. We got it at Sears. Do you remember when Sears used to be the place to go for everything? We bought our first refrigerator there, too. A big avocado green thing. It matched the stove perfectly. That was when everyone was doing their kitchens in avocado green or harvest gold. We had this terrible floral wallpaper to go with it... I finally made Arthur help me steam it off about ten years later, and what a mess that was. Little pieces of paper stuck to our shoes for weeks... \n\nBut, oh, my weight. No, I haven't noticed any change. I just haven't been paying attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your husband's collapse or anything else?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I... I don't think so. I mean, it happened so fast. One minute he was just sitting there in his favorite recliner—the brown leather one, you know? We got that chair at a discount furniture place out on Route 9. The man there, I think his name was Sal, he gave us such a good deal because there was a tiny scratch on the back leg. You couldn't even see it unless you got down on your hands and knees. Arthur loved that chair. He'd sit there every evening watching Jeopardy, shouting out the answers. He was always so good at history questions, but he couldn't get the pop culture ones to save his life. He didn't know who Madonna was until the late nineties... \n\nBut, no, doctor. I don't feel guilty. I just... I don't feel much of anything at all.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No... no, I don't think so. I was just looking out the window this morning, at my garden. The sun was hitting the hydrangeas just right. Arthur and I planted those hydrangeas when we first moved into the house. Oh, they must be forty years old now. We had to dig up this awful rocky soil to get them in, and Arthur broke his favorite trowel doing it. He was so mad about that trowel, he tried to fix it with duct tape, but it never held. He was always trying to fix things with duct tape. He taped up the handle on the lawnmower, the hinge on the screen door... he even tried to fix a leaky pipe under the sink once. Let me tell you, that was a disaster. We had water all over the linoleum, and we had to call a plumber anyway. The plumber, a nice young man named Ricky, charged us an arm and a leg, but he said Arthur's tape job was the funniest thing he'd ever seen...\n\nBut the garden... yes. The sun was on it, and it looked so pretty, but I just... I felt flat inside. Even when the kids sit with me, like Susan yesterday, it's the same. It's like the part of me that could feel glad about the flowers, or comforted by my children... it just shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself since your husband's collapse?\n\nPatient: Hurting myself? Oh, heavens, no. I haven't thought about anything like that. I wouldn't even know how to... well, I suppose people do all sorts of things. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable—she lived two houses down, the one with the big oak tree in the front yard that drops acorns all over the sidewalk every fall. Arthur used to spend hours sweeping those acorns up. He said if he didn't, someone would slip and break their neck, and then we'd be sued. He was always worrying about being sued. He bought this special push broom just for the acorns, with these stiff red bristles. He got it at the hardware store downtown, the one that closed when the big home improvement place opened out by the highway. It was a shame, really. The man who owned it, Mr. Henderson, he always gave the neighborhood kids a free piece of hard candy from a glass jar on the counter. Peppermint, usually, or butterscotch. Arthur loved butterscotch. He kept a roll of those little yellow candies in the glove compartment of the car, and they'd get all melted together in the summer...\n\nBut no, doctor. No thoughts like that. I'm just sitting here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience any pleasure at all, even in small amounts?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No, I... I don't think I can. I mean, I was just saying about the garden, wasn't I? The sun was shining on it, and it was so pretty, but I just felt... flat. Empty, really. And it's the same with my children. Susan came over yesterday. She brought over a casserole, one of those tuna noodle ones with the crushed potato chips on top. I used to make those for the kids when they were little. Arthur hated it, though. He always said tuna should be in a sandwich with mayonnaise and a piece of crisp lettuce, not baked in an oven. He was very particular about his sandwiches. He'd only eat that one brand of bread, the kind in the yellow wrapper with the red letters. If I bought the store brand, he'd know right away. He said the texture was all wrong, like eating a sponge. He could be so stubborn about food. Once, we went to this Italian restaurant for our anniversary, and he sent his spaghetti back three times because the sauce wasn't hot enough... \n\nBut, yes... even when Susan is sitting right there next to me, or when I'm looking at the flowers, it's just... nothing. It's like that part of me, the part that used to feel glad or comforted, it just completely shut off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

He got it at the hardware store downtown, the one that closed when the big home improvement place opened out by the highway. It was a shame, really. The man who owned it, Mr. Henderson, he always gave the neighborhood kids a free piece of hard candy from a glass jar on the counter. Peppermint, usually, or butterscotch. Arthur loved butterscotch. He kept a roll of those little yellow candies in the glove compartment of the car, and they'd get all melted together in the summer...\n\nBut no, doctor. No thoughts like that. I'm just sitting here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience any pleasure at all, even in small amounts?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No, I... I don't think I can. I mean, I was just saying about the garden, wasn't I? The sun was shining on it, and it was so pretty, but I just felt... flat. Empty, really. And it's the same with my children. Susan came over yesterday. She brought over a casserole, one of those tuna noodle ones with the crushed potato chips on top. I used to make those for the kids when they were little. Arthur hated it, though. He always said tuna should be in a sandwich with mayonnaise and a piece of crisp lettuce, not baked in an oven. He was very particular about his sandwiches. He'd only eat that one brand of bread, the kind in the yellow wrapper with the red letters. If I bought the store brand, he'd know right away. He said the texture was all wrong, like eating a sponge. He could be so stubborn about food. Once, we went to this Italian restaurant for our anniversary, and he sent his spaghetti back three times because the sauce wasn't hot enough... \n\nBut, yes... even when Susan is sitting right there next to me, or when I'm looking at the flowers, it's just... nothing. It's like that part of me, the part that used to feel glad or comforted, it just completely shut off.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you've lost interest in almost all activities you used to enjoy?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Interest? Oh... yes, I suppose I have. I just don't have the energy for anything anymore. I used to knit, you know. I knitted all the time. Sweaters, scarves, little booties for the grandbabies when they came along. I had this beautiful basket next to my armchair, woven from willow branches. I bought it at a craft fair up in Vermont... oh, it must have been thirty years ago. Arthur and I took a trip up there to see the fall leaves. The colors were just spectacular that year, bright reds and oranges, like the whole mountain was on fire. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast that smelled like cinnamon and woodsmoke. The lady who ran it, Mrs. Higgins, she made the most wonderful blueberry pancakes. Arthur ate a whole stack of them by himself. He spilled syrup all down the front of his favorite flannel shirt—the green plaid one. I tried everything to get that stain out, soaking it, scrubbing it with that strong soap, but it always left a little shadow. He wore it anyway, right up until the elbows wore through... 

But knitting... no, I haven't picked up my needles since... since it happened. I just look at the basket and I can't even imagine wanting to make anything.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor... I came because... I just don't feel right anymore. Even when my children come over and sit with me on the porch, or when the sun is shining so beautifully on my garden—oh, you should see my hydrangeas this year, they finally bloomed after I moved them to the east side of the house. I always told my late husband they needed morning sun, but he insisted on planting them by the oak tree. He was stubborn like that, bless him. He bought that oak tree as a sapling back in '78... \n\nBut anyway, what I mean is, even when I'm looking at those flowers or sitting with my kids, I just feel completely flat inside. It's like the part of me that used to feel glad or... or comforted by those things just completely shut off. I don't know where the feeling went.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's been... I'd say about two weeks now. Yes, just about two weeks. It started right after my husband collapsed. He was right there in the kitchen, and I... I was making a pot roast. You know, the butcher down at the corner market—well, his son runs it mostly now, but he always saves the best chuck roasts for me because I've been shopping there since 1985. The prices have gone up terribly, of course. I nearly fainted when I saw what they charge for a dozen eggs lately, it's just absolute robbery... \n\nBut anyway, I was standing at the stove, and Arthur just went down. And then the paramedics came, and they were working on him right there on my kitchen floor. They had these big heavy boots on, and I remember thinking about how they tracked in so much dirt on my nice linoleum... they worked on him right between the island and the sink. So yes, ever since that happened two weeks ago, I've just been feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to enjoy activities or hobbies that you used to find pleasurable before these past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying about the garden... no. Not at all. I try, I really do. I used to love knitting, too, besides the gardening. I was right in the middle of making a nice blue cardigan for my grandson, David. He's at the state university now, studying civil engineering. Such a smart boy. His father—my son, Richard—really wanted him to go into business, but David has always been so good with his hands. He used to take apart my toaster when he was just a little thing, leaving springs and screws all over the kitchen table... I was always finding pieces of it for months. \n\nBut, oh, what were you asking... right, the hobbies. No, doctor. I pick up those knitting needles now, or I go sit out by those hydrangeas, and it's exactly like I was just telling you. I just feel completely flat inside. That warm, happy feeling I used to get when I finished a row of stitches or when the kids come to visit... it's just gone. It's like the switch for feeling glad about anything has just been completely turned off.\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel a sense of connection or pleasure when spending time with your children, or does that also feel flat to you?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, it just... it feels completely flat. Even with my children. They try to be so sweet, coming over and checking on me. My daughter Susan brought over a casserole just yesterday. She makes it with those little crispy fried onions on top, you know the ones that come in the can? I used to make it for Arthur every Thanksgiving. He wouldn't eat the green beans unless they were smothered in cream of mushroom soup and those onions. Thanksgiving was always such a big production in our house. We'd have twenty people sometimes, back when Arthur's brothers were still alive. We'd have to set up two card tables in the living room just to fit everyone, and the noise... oh, the noise was something else. \n\nBut... yes, to answer your question, when Susan brought that casserole over and sat with me, I just... I didn't feel anything. I knew I should feel grateful, or happy to see her, but it was just... nothing. Just flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Weight? Oh, I don't really know. I haven't stepped on a scale in... well, it's been ages. I used to have one of those nice mechanical ones, the kind with the little dial that spins around. Arthur bought it for me back in, oh, it must have been 1998. We got it at Sears. Do you remember when Sears used to be the place to go for everything? We bought our first refrigerator there, too. A big avocado green thing. It matched the stove perfectly. That was when everyone was doing their kitchens in avocado green or harvest gold. We had this terrible floral wallpaper to go with it... I finally made Arthur help me steam it off about ten years later, and what a mess that was. Little pieces of paper stuck to our shoes for weeks... \n\nBut, oh, my weight. No, I haven't noticed any change. I just haven't been paying attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your husband's collapse or anything else?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I... I don't think so. I mean, it happened so fast. One minute he was just sitting there in his favorite recliner—the brown leather one, you know? We got that chair at a discount furniture place out on Route 9. The man there, I think his name was Sal, he gave us such a good deal because there was a tiny scratch on the back leg. You couldn't even see it unless you got down on your hands and knees. Arthur loved that chair. He'd sit there every evening watching Jeopardy, shouting out the answers. He was always so good at history questions, but he couldn't get the pop culture ones to save his life. He didn't know who Madonna was until the late nineties... \n\nBut, no, doctor. I don't feel guilty. I just... I don't feel much of anything at all.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No... no, I don't think so. I was just looking out the window this morning, at my garden. The sun was hitting the hydrangeas just right. He was always trying to fix things with duct tape. He taped up the handle on the lawnmower, the hinge on the screen door... he even tried to fix a leaky pipe under the sink once. It's like the part of me that could feel glad about the flowers, or comforted by my children... it just shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself since your husband's collapse?\n\nPatient: Hurting myself? Oh, heavens, no. I haven't thought about anything like that. I wouldn't even know how to... well, I suppose people do all sorts of things. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable—she lived two houses down, the one with the big oak tree in the front yard that drops acorns all over the sidewalk every fall. Arthur used to spend hours sweeping those acorns up. He said if he didn't, someone would slip and break their neck, and then we'd be sued. He was always worrying about being sued. He bought this special push broom just for the acorns, with these stiff red bristles. He got it at the hardware store downtown, the one that closed when the big home improvement place opened out by the highway. It was a shame, really. The man who owned it, Mr. Henderson, he always gave the neighborhood kids a free piece of hard candy from a glass jar on the counter. Peppermint, usually, or butterscotch. Arthur loved butterscotch. He kept a roll of those little yellow candies in the glove compartment of the car, and they'd get all melted together in the summer...\n\nBut no, doctor. No thoughts like that. I'm just sitting here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience any pleasure at all, even in small amounts?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No, I... I don't think I can. I mean, I was just saying about the garden, wasn't I? The sun was shining on it, and it was so pretty, but I just felt... flat. Empty, really. And it's the same with my children. Susan came over yesterday. She brought over a casserole, one of those tuna noodle ones with the crushed potato chips on top. I used to make those for the kids when they were little. Arthur hated it, though. He always said tuna should be in a sandwich with mayonnaise and a piece of crisp lettuce, not baked in an oven. He was very particular about his sandwiches. He'd only eat that one brand of bread, the kind in the yellow wrapper with the red letters. If I bought the store brand, he'd know right away. He said the texture was all wrong, like eating a sponge. He could be so stubborn about food. Once, we went to this Italian restaurant for our anniversary, and he sent his spaghetti back three times because the sauce wasn't hot enough... \n\nBut, yes... even when Susan is sitting right there next to me, or when I'm looking at the flowers, it's just... nothing. It's like that part of me, the part that used to feel glad or comforted, it just completely shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in almost all activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Interest? Oh... yes, I suppose I have. I just don't have the energy for anything anymore. I used to knit, you know. I knitted all the time. Sweaters, scarves, little booties for the grandbabies when they came along. I had this beautiful basket next to my armchair, woven from willow branches. I bought it at a craft fair up in Vermont... oh, it must have been thirty years ago. Arthur and I took a trip up there to see the fall leaves. The colors were just spectacular that year, bright reds and oranges, like the whole mountain was on fire. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast that smelled like cinnamon and woodsmoke. The lady who ran it, Mrs. Higgins, she made the most wonderful blueberry pancakes. Arthur ate a whole stack of them by himself. He spilled syrup all down the front of his favorite flannel shirt—the green plaid one. I tried everything to get that stain out, soaking it, scrubbing it with that strong soap, but it always left a little shadow. He wore it anyway, right up until the elbows wore through... \n\nBut knitting... no, I haven't picked up my needles since... since it happened. I just look at the basket and I can't even imagine wanting to make anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Pleasure? I... I thought we just talked about this, doctor. But, no. No, I don't feel anything like that. Not even for a second. It's just... flat. Like I was saying about the garden, and Susan coming over. She's such a good girl, Susan. She works so hard at that real estate office. Always showing houses on the weekends. I don't know how she manages it with the two boys. Timmy, the older one, he just started playing baseball. Little League. He's a catcher. Arthur would have loved that. Arthur played baseball in high school, you know. He was a pitcher. He had this old leather mitt he kept in a box in the attic. Sometimes he'd get it out and rub it down with this special oil that smelled like pine needles. He said you had to take care of leather or it would dry out and crack. Just like skin, he'd say. He had very dry hands in the winter. He'd put on that thick white lotion before bed, the kind that comes in the blue jar, and he'd wear white cotton gloves to sleep so it wouldn't get all over the sheets. I used to tease him about looking like a magician in bed...

But no... to answer your question, doctor. No pleasure at all. Not even for a moment.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor... I came because... I just don't feel right anymore. Even when my children come over and sit with me on the porch, or when the sun is shining so beautifully on my garden—oh, you should see my hydrangeas this year, they finally bloomed after I moved them to the east side of the house. I always told my late husband they needed morning sun, but he insisted on planting them by the oak tree. He was stubborn like that, bless him. He bought that oak tree as a sapling back in '78... \n\nBut anyway, what I mean is, even when I'm looking at those flowers or sitting with my kids, I just feel completely flat inside. It's like the part of me that used to feel glad or... or comforted by those things just completely shut off. I don't know where the feeling went.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's been... I'd say about two weeks now. Yes, just about two weeks. It started right after my husband collapsed. He was right there in the kitchen, and I... I was making a pot roast. You know, the butcher down at the corner market—well, his son runs it mostly now, but he always saves the best chuck roasts for me because I've been shopping there since 1985. The prices have gone up terribly, of course. I nearly fainted when I saw what they charge for a dozen eggs lately, it's just absolute robbery... \n\nBut anyway, I was standing at the stove, and Arthur just went down. And then the paramedics came, and they were working on him right there on my kitchen floor. They had these big heavy boots on, and I remember thinking about how they tracked in so much dirt on my nice linoleum... they worked on him right between the island and the sink. So yes, ever since that happened two weeks ago, I've just been feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to enjoy activities or hobbies that you used to find pleasurable before these past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying about the garden... no. Not at all. I try, I really do. I used to love knitting, too, besides the gardening. I was right in the middle of making a nice blue cardigan for my grandson, David. He's at the state university now, studying civil engineering. Such a smart boy. His father—my son, Richard—really wanted him to go into business, but David has always been so good with his hands. He used to take apart my toaster when he was just a little thing, leaving springs and screws all over the kitchen table... I was always finding pieces of it for months. \n\nBut, oh, what were you asking... right, the hobbies. No, doctor. I pick up those knitting needles now, or I go sit out by those hydrangeas, and it's exactly like I was just telling you. I just feel completely flat inside. That warm, happy feeling I used to get when I finished a row of stitches or when the kids come to visit... it's just gone. It's like the switch for feeling glad about anything has just been completely turned off.\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel a sense of connection or pleasure when spending time with your children, or does that also feel flat to you?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, it just... it feels completely flat. Even with my children. They try to be so sweet, coming over and checking on me. My daughter Susan brought over a casserole just yesterday. She makes it with those little crispy fried onions on top, you know the ones that come in the can? I used to make it for Arthur every Thanksgiving. He wouldn't eat the green beans unless they were smothered in cream of mushroom soup and those onions. Thanksgiving was always such a big production in our house. We'd have twenty people sometimes, back when Arthur's brothers were still alive. We'd have to set up two card tables in the living room just to fit everyone, and the noise... oh, the noise was something else. \n\nBut... yes, to answer your question, when Susan brought that casserole over and sat with me, I just... I didn't feel anything. I knew I should feel grateful, or happy to see her, but it was just... nothing. Just flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Weight? Oh, I don't really know. I haven't stepped on a scale in... well, it's been ages. I used to have one of those nice mechanical ones, the kind with the little dial that spins around. Arthur bought it for me back in, oh, it must have been 1998. We got it at Sears. Do you remember when Sears used to be the place to go for everything? We bought our first refrigerator there, too. A big avocado green thing. It matched the stove perfectly. That was when everyone was doing their kitchens in avocado green or harvest gold. We had this terrible floral wallpaper to go with it... I finally made Arthur help me steam it off about ten years later, and what a mess that was. Little pieces of paper stuck to our shoes for weeks... \n\nBut, oh, my weight. No, I haven't noticed any change. I just haven't been paying attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your husband's collapse or anything else?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I... I don't think so. I mean, it happened so fast. One minute he was just sitting there in his favorite recliner—the brown leather one, you know? We got that chair at a discount furniture place out on Route 9. The man there, I think his name was Sal, he gave us such a good deal because there was a tiny scratch on the back leg. You couldn't even see it unless you got down on your hands and knees. Arthur loved that chair. He'd sit there every evening watching Jeopardy, shouting out the answers. He was always so good at history questions, but he couldn't get the pop culture ones to save his life. He didn't know who Madonna was until the late nineties... \n\nBut, no, doctor. I don't feel guilty. I just... I don't feel much of anything at all.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No... no, I don't think so. I was just looking out the window this morning, at my garden. The sun was hitting the hydrangeas just right. Arthur and I planted those hydrangeas when we first moved into the house. Oh, they must be forty years old now. We had to dig up this awful rocky soil to get them in, and Arthur broke his favorite trowel doing it. He was so mad about that trowel, he tried to fix it with duct tape, but it never held. He was always trying to fix things with duct tape. He taped up the handle on the lawnmower, the hinge on the screen door... he even tried to fix a leaky pipe under the sink once. Let me tell you, that was a disaster. We had water all over the linoleum, and we had to call a plumber anyway. The plumber, a nice young man named Ricky, charged us an arm and a leg, but he said Arthur's tape job was the funniest thing he'd ever seen...\n\nBut the garden... yes. The sun was on it, and it looked so pretty, but I just... I felt flat inside. Even when the kids sit with me, like Susan yesterday, it's the same. It's like the part of me that could feel glad about the flowers, or comforted by my children... it just shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself since your husband's collapse?\n\nPatient: Hurting myself? Oh, heavens, no. I haven't thought about anything like that. I wouldn't even know how to... well, I suppose people do all sorts of things. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable—she lived two houses down, the one with the big oak tree in the front yard that drops acorns all over the sidewalk every fall. Arthur used to spend hours sweeping those acorns up. He said if he didn't, someone would slip and break their neck, and then we'd be sued. He was always worrying about being sued. He bought this special push broom just for the acorns, with these stiff red bristles. He got it at the hardware store downtown, the one that closed when the big home improvement place opened out by the highway. It was a shame, really. The man who owned it, Mr. Henderson, he always gave the neighborhood kids a free piece of hard candy from a glass jar on the counter. Peppermint, usually, or butterscotch. Arthur loved butterscotch. He kept a roll of those little yellow candies in the glove compartment of the car, and they'd get all melted together in the summer...\n\nBut no, doctor. No thoughts like that. I'm just sitting here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience any pleasure at all, even in small amounts?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No, I... I don't think I can. I mean, I was just saying about the garden, wasn't I? The sun was shining on it, and it was so pretty, but I just felt... flat. Empty, really. And it's the same with my children. Susan came over yesterday. She brought over a casserole, one of those tuna noodle ones with the crushed potato chips on top. I used to make those for the kids when they were little. Arthur hated it, though. He always said tuna should be in a sandwich with mayonnaise and a piece of crisp lettuce, not baked in an oven. He was very particular about his sandwiches. He'd only eat that one brand of bread, the kind in the yellow wrapper with the red letters. If I bought the store brand, he'd know right away. He said the texture was all wrong, like eating a sponge. He could be so stubborn about food. I bought it at a craft fair up in Vermont... oh, it must have been thirty years ago. Arthur and I took a trip up there to see the fall leaves. The colors were just spectacular that year, bright reds and oranges, like the whole mountain was on fire. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast that smelled like cinnamon and woodsmoke. He spilled syrup all down the front of his favorite flannel shirt—the green plaid one. I tried everything to get that stain out, soaking it, scrubbing it with that strong soap, but it always left a little shadow. He wore it anyway, right up until the elbows wore through... \n\nBut knitting... no, I haven't picked up my needles since... since it happened. I just look at the basket and I can't even imagine wanting to make anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience any pleasure at all, even in very small amounts or for a brief moment?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? Timmy, the older one, he just started playing baseball. Little League. He's a catcher. Arthur would have loved that. Arthur played baseball in high school, you know. He was a pitcher. He had this old leather mitt he kept in a box in the attic. Sometimes he'd get it out and rub it down with this special oil that smelled like pine needles. He said you had to take care of leather or it would dry out and crack. Just like skin, he'd say. He had very dry hands in the winter. Not even for a moment.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're unable to experience any pleasure at all, even in things that you would normally find very small amounts of joy in?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 81 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Inability_Positive_Emotions\nmanifestation: \"I came because even when my children sit with me or the sun is on my garden, I feel flat inside, like the part of me that could be glad or comforted just shut off.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... I feel like we're going in circles, doctor. I keep trying to explain it. I came here because... well, like I said, Susan sits right there with me, or I look out at the garden when the sun is shining so pretty on it, and there's just... nothing. I feel completely flat inside. It's exactly like that part of me, the part that could be glad or comforted by those things, just shut off entirely. 

It reminds me of when the electricity goes out during a winter storm. We had this terrible ice storm back in '98. The power lines were down for a week. The house got so cold we had to sleep in our winter coats in the living room, huddled around the fireplace. Arthur kept feeding the fire with these big oak logs he'd chopped in the fall. He was so proud of that woodpile. He stacked it perfectly behind the shed, alternating the direction of the logs so it would breathe and dry out properly. He said if you don't stack it right, it rots from the inside out. He had this heavy axe with a hickory handle... he kept the blade so sharp you could shave with it, he used to joke. He'd spend hours out there on Saturday mornings, just chopping wood. The sound of it... thwack, thwack... it would echo all through the neighborhood...

But, yes. Flat inside. That's exactly how it is. No joy at all, not even a little bit.
