Well, yes, it... it really is affecting everything. I've completely stopped going down to the senior center. I used to go every Tuesday and Thursday for the knitting circle. We were all working on these little caps for the premature babies at the hospital. Helen always brought her famous lemon squares... oh, they were so tart they'd make your jaw ache, but you couldn't eat just one. She used the lemons right off the tree in her daughter's backyard in Florida. Her daughter moved down there... oh, it must be fifteen years ago now. Married a man who sold boats or something like that. They have a lovely house with a pool enclosed in one of those big screens to keep the mosquitoes out... 

But I just can't bring myself to go anymore. I can't even manage the simple things here at home. Meals, running errands, keeping track of my calendar... my daughter has had to step in and do all of it for me. I mostly just stay in the house now. And I... I try to avoid the kitchen completely unless someone is in there with me. I just can't be in there alone. Not since Arthur...

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor... I came because... I just don't feel right anymore. Even when my children come over and sit with me on the porch, or when the sun is shining so beautifully on my garden—oh, you should see my hydrangeas this year, they finally bloomed after I moved them to the east side of the house. I always told my late husband they needed morning sun, but he insisted on planting them by the oak tree. He was stubborn like that, bless him. He bought that oak tree as a sapling back in '78... \n\nBut anyway, what I mean is, even when I'm looking at those flowers or sitting with my kids, I just feel completely flat inside. It's like the part of me that used to feel glad or... or comforted by those things just completely shut off. It started right after my husband collapsed. He was right there in the kitchen, and I... I was making a pot roast. The prices have gone up terribly, of course. I nearly fainted when I saw what they charge for a dozen eggs lately, it's just absolute robbery... \n\nBut anyway, I was standing at the stove, and Arthur just went down. Not at all. I try, I really do. I used to love knitting, too, besides the gardening. I was right in the middle of making a nice blue cardigan for my grandson, David. He's at the state university now, studying civil engineering. Such a smart boy. His father—my son, Richard—really wanted him to go into business, but David has always been so good with his hands. He used to take apart my toaster when he was just a little thing, leaving springs and screws all over the kitchen table... I was always finding pieces of it for months. \n\nBut, oh, what were you asking... right, the hobbies. No, doctor. I pick up those knitting needles now, or I go sit out by those hydrangeas, and it's exactly like I was just telling you. I just feel completely flat inside. That warm, happy feeling I used to get when I finished a row of stitches or when the kids come to visit... it's just gone. It's like the switch for feeling glad about anything has just been completely turned off.\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel a sense of connection or pleasure when spending time with your children, or does that also feel flat to you?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, it just... it feels completely flat. Even with my children. They try to be so sweet, coming over and checking on me. My daughter Susan brought over a casserole just yesterday. She makes it with those little crispy fried onions on top, you know the ones that come in the can? I used to make it for Arthur every Thanksgiving. He wouldn't eat the green beans unless they were smothered in cream of mushroom soup and those onions. Thanksgiving was always such a big production in our house. We'd have twenty people sometimes, back when Arthur's brothers were still alive. We'd have to set up two card tables in the living room just to fit everyone, and the noise... oh, the noise was something else. \n\nBut... yes, to answer your question, when Susan brought that casserole over and sat with me, I just... I didn't feel anything. I knew I should feel grateful, or happy to see her, but it was just... nothing. Just flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Weight? Oh, I don't really know. I haven't stepped on a scale in... well, it's been ages. I used to have one of those nice mechanical ones, the kind with the little dial that spins around. Arthur bought it for me back in, oh, it must have been 1998. We got it at Sears. Do you remember when Sears used to be the place to go for everything? We bought our first refrigerator there, too. A big avocado green thing. It matched the stove perfectly. That was when everyone was doing their kitchens in avocado green or harvest gold. We had this terrible floral wallpaper to go with it... I finally made Arthur help me steam it off about ten years later, and what a mess that was. Little pieces of paper stuck to our shoes for weeks... \n\nBut, oh, my weight. No, I haven't noticed any change. I just haven't been paying attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your husband's collapse or anything else?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I... I don't think so. I mean, it happened so fast. One minute he was just sitting there in his favorite recliner—the brown leather one, you know? We got that chair at a discount furniture place out on Route 9. The man there, I think his name was Sal, he gave us such a good deal because there was a tiny scratch on the back leg. You couldn't even see it unless you got down on your hands and knees. Arthur loved that chair. He'd sit there every evening watching Jeopardy, shouting out the answers. He was always so good at history questions, but he couldn't get the pop culture ones to save his life. He didn't know who Madonna was until the late nineties... \n\nBut, no, doctor. I don't feel guilty. I just... I don't feel much of anything at all.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No... no, I don't think so. I was just looking out the window this morning, at my garden. The sun was hitting the hydrangeas just right. Arthur and I planted those hydrangeas when we first moved into the house. Oh, they must be forty years old now. We had to dig up this awful rocky soil to get them in, and Arthur broke his favorite trowel doing it. He was so mad about that trowel, he tried to fix it with duct tape, but it never held. He was always trying to fix things with duct tape. He taped up the handle on the lawnmower, the hinge on the screen door... he even tried to fix a leaky pipe under the sink once. Let me tell you, that was a disaster. We had water all over the linoleum, and we had to call a plumber anyway. The plumber, a nice young man named Ricky, charged us an arm and a leg, but he said Arthur's tape job was the funniest thing he'd ever seen...\n\nBut the garden... yes. The sun was on it, and it looked so pretty, but I just... I felt flat inside. Even when the kids sit with me, like Susan yesterday, it's the same. It's like the part of me that could feel glad about the flowers, or comforted by my children... it just shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself since your husband's collapse?\n\nPatient: Hurting myself? Oh, heavens, no. I haven't thought about anything like that. I wouldn't even know how to... well, I suppose people do all sorts of things. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable—she lived two houses down, the one with the big oak tree in the front yard that drops acorns all over the sidewalk every fall. Arthur used to spend hours sweeping those acorns up. He said if he didn't, someone would slip and break their neck, and then we'd be sued. He was always worrying about being sued. He bought this special push broom just for the acorns, with these stiff red bristles. He got it at the hardware store downtown, the one that closed when the big home improvement place opened out by the highway. It was a shame, really. The man who owned it, Mr. Henderson, he always gave the neighborhood kids a free piece of hard candy from a glass jar on the counter. Peppermint, usually, or butterscotch. Arthur loved butterscotch. He kept a roll of those little yellow candies in the glove compartment of the car, and they'd get all melted together in the summer...\n\nBut no, doctor. No thoughts like that. I'm just sitting here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience any pleasure at all, even in small amounts?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No, I... I don't think I can. I mean, I was just saying about the garden, wasn't I? The sun was shining on it, and it was so pretty, but I just felt... flat. Empty, really. And it's the same with my children. Susan came over yesterday. She brought over a casserole, one of those tuna noodle ones with the crushed potato chips on top. I used to make those for the kids when they were little. Arthur hated it, though. He always said tuna should be in a sandwich with mayonnaise and a piece of crisp lettuce, not baked in an oven. He was very particular about his sandwiches. He'd only eat that one brand of bread, the kind in the yellow wrapper with the red letters. If I bought the store brand, he'd know right away. He said the texture was all wrong, like eating a sponge. He could be so stubborn about food. Once, we went to this Italian restaurant for our anniversary, and he sent his spaghetti back three times because the sauce wasn't hot enough... \n\nBut, yes... even when Susan is sitting right there next to me, or when I'm looking at the flowers, it's just... nothing. It's like that part of me, the part that used to feel glad or comforted, it just completely shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in almost all activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Interest? Oh... yes, I suppose I have. I just don't have the energy for anything anymore. I used to knit, you know. I knitted all the time. Sweaters, scarves, little booties for the grandbabies when they came along. I had this beautiful basket next to my armchair, woven from willow branches. I bought it at a craft fair up in Vermont... oh, it must have been thirty years ago. Arthur and I took a trip up there to see the fall leaves. The colors were just spectacular that year, bright reds and oranges, like the whole mountain was on fire. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast that smelled like cinnamon and woodsmoke. The lady who ran it, Mrs. Higgins, she made the most wonderful blueberry pancakes. Arthur ate a whole stack of them by himself. He spilled syrup all down the front of his favorite flannel shirt—the green plaid one. I tried everything to get that stain out, soaking it, scrubbing it with that strong soap, but it always left a little shadow. He wore it anyway, right up until the elbows wore through... \n\nBut knitting... no, I haven't picked up my needles since... since it happened. I just look at the basket and I can't even imagine wanting to make anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience any pleasure at all, even in very small amounts or for a brief moment?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? I... I thought we just talked about this, doctor. But, no. No, I don't feel anything like that. Not even for a second. It's just... flat. Like I was saying about the garden, and Susan coming over. She's such a good girl, Susan. She works so hard at that real estate office. Always showing houses on the weekends. I don't know how she manages it with the two boys. Timmy, the older one, he just started playing baseball. Little League. He's a catcher. Arthur would have loved that. Arthur played baseball in high school, you know. He was a pitcher. He had this old leather mitt he kept in a box in the attic. Sometimes he'd get it out and rub it down with this special oil that smelled like pine needles. He said you had to take care of leather or it would dry out and crack. Just like skin, he'd say. He had very dry hands in the winter. He'd put on that thick white lotion before bed, the kind that comes in the blue jar, and he'd wear white cotton gloves to sleep so it wouldn't get all over the sheets. I used to tease him about looking like a magician in bed...\n\nBut no... to answer your question, doctor. No pleasure at all. Not even for a moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience any pleasure at all, even in things that you would normally find very small amounts of joy in?\n\nPatient: I... I feel like we're going in circles, doctor. I keep trying to explain it. I came here because... well, like I said, Susan sits right there with me, or I look out at the garden when the sun is shining so pretty on it, and there's just... nothing. I feel completely flat inside. It's exactly like that part of me, the part that could be glad or comforted by those things, just shut off entirely. \n\nIt reminds me of when the electricity goes out during a winter storm. We had this terrible ice storm back in '98. The power lines were down for a week. The house got so cold we had to sleep in our winter coats in the living room, huddled around the fireplace. Arthur kept feeding the fire with these big oak logs he'd chopped in the fall. He was so proud of that woodpile. He stacked it perfectly behind the shed, alternating the direction of the logs so it would breathe and dry out properly. He said if you don't stack it right, it rots from the inside out. He had this heavy axe with a hickory handle... he kept the blade so sharp you could shave with it, he used to joke. He'd spend hours out there on Saturday mornings, just chopping wood. The sound of it... thwack, thwack... it would echo all through the neighborhood...\n\nBut, yes. Flat inside. That's exactly how it is. No joy at all, not even a little bit.\n\nDoctor: How many days in a row have you been feeling this flatness inside?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been about two weeks now. Ever since Arthur... ever since he collapsed. Right there on the kitchen floor. The paramedics, they came rushing in through the back door... the screen door slammed so hard it knocked the little wooden sign off the wall. The one that says \"Bless this Mess.\" My sister gave me that sign when we first bought the house. She found it at a little antique shop in Cape Cod. We used to go to the Cape every summer when we were girls. We'd rent this tiny cottage near the beach, and the sand would get into absolutely everything. Our beds, our shoes, even the sandwiches my mother packed for lunch. She always made egg salad sandwiches on white bread, wrapped tight in wax paper. I can still taste the crunch of the sand mixed with the egg salad...\n\nBut yes, two weeks. Ever since they were working on him right there on the linoleum. It's been exactly two weeks of just... nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this flatness inside is something that you cannot escape, no matter what you are doing?\n\nPatient: Yes, I can't escape it at all. Like I keep saying, that's exactly why I came to see you in the first place. Even when my children sit right there with me, or the sun is shining so beautifully on my garden, I just feel completely flat inside. It's like the part of me that could be glad or comforted by any of it just shut off entirely. \n\nJust... click, and it's gone. Like a broken light switch. We had a broken switch in the hallway for the longest time, right by the coat closet. Arthur kept meaning to fix it. He bought the replacement part at Miller's Hardware downtown—the old one with the squeaky wooden floors. Mr. Miller used to give out peppermint candies from a big glass jar by the cash register. They were those soft peppermints, the ones that just melt in your mouth, wrapped in clear plastic with little red stripes. I always kept a few in my pocketbook for Sunday service. Reverend Thomas would preach for over an hour sometimes, and you just needed a little something sweet to keep your throat from getting dry. He had this booming voice that would just rattle the stained glass windows, especially when he got worked up about the Old Testament... \n\nBut, yes. I can't escape the flatness. No matter what I'm doing, that part of me just stays shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the flatness inside is getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: I don't... I don't know if it's getting worse. It's just... there. It's just always there, this heavy, empty feeling. It doesn't seem to get better or worse, it just stays the same. \n\nIt's like... it's like that old grandfather clock we have in the hallway. The one Arthur's father left him. It just ticks and ticks, the exact same rhythm, day in and day out. Tick, tock, tick, tock. Never faster, never slower. It's made of this beautiful dark mahogany. Arthur used to polish it every Sunday afternoon with this special beeswax polish that smelled like lemons and old wood. He'd rub it in tiny little circles with an old flannel shirt he'd cut up into rags. He wouldn't let me use any of the good dust cloths on it, said they'd scratch the finish. He was so particular about his rags. He kept them in a coffee can in the garage—an old Maxwell House can, the blue one with the plastic lid. We used to drink Maxwell House every morning, brewed in this percolator that made the whole kitchen smell like heaven...\n\nBut the flatness... no, I haven't noticed it getting worse. It's just constant. Just... always exactly the same.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the flatness inside is affecting your daily life and ability to function?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it... it really is affecting everything. I've completely stopped going down to the senior center. I used to go every Tuesday and Thursday for the knitting circle. We were all working on these little caps for the premature babies at the hospital. Helen always brought her famous lemon squares... oh, they were so tart they'd make your jaw ache, but you couldn't eat just one. She used the lemons right off the tree in her daughter's backyard in Florida. Her daughter moved down there... oh, it must be fifteen years ago now. Married a man who sold boats or something like that. They have a lovely house with a pool enclosed in one of those big screens to keep the mosquitoes out... \n\nBut I just can't bring myself to go anymore. I can't even manage the simple things here at home. Meals, running errands, keeping track of my calendar... my daughter has had to step in and do all of it for me. I mostly just stay in the house now. And I... I try to avoid the kitchen completely unless someone is in there with me. I just can't be in there alone. Not since Arthur...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

He was so particular about his rags. He kept them in a coffee can in the garage—an old Maxwell House can, the blue one with the plastic lid. We used to drink Maxwell House every morning, brewed in this percolator that made the whole kitchen smell like heaven...\n\nBut the flatness... no, I haven't noticed it getting worse. It's just constant. Just... always exactly the same.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the flatness inside is affecting your daily life and ability to function?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it... it really is affecting everything. I've completely stopped going down to the senior center. I used to go every Tuesday and Thursday for the knitting circle. We were all working on these little caps for the premature babies at the hospital. Helen always brought her famous lemon squares... oh, they were so tart they'd make your jaw ache, but you couldn't eat just one. She used the lemons right off the tree in her daughter's backyard in Florida. Her daughter moved down there... oh, it must be fifteen years ago now. Married a man who sold boats or something like that. They have a lovely house with a pool enclosed in one of those big screens to keep the mosquitoes out... \n\nBut I just can't bring myself to go anymore. I can't even manage the simple things here at home. Meals, running errands, keeping track of my calendar... my daughter has had to step in and do all of it for me. I mostly just stay in the house now. And I... I try to avoid the kitchen completely unless someone is in there with me. I just can't be in there alone. Not since Arthur...\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like the flatness inside is making it hard for you to take care of your basic needs?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, that's... that's exactly what I was just trying to say a moment ago. I just can't do any of it. My daughter has to manage all my meals and run all my errands now because I just stay shut up in the house. I don't even look at my calendar anymore to know what day it is. And like I said, I won't even go into the kitchen to fix myself a simple piece of toast unless she's standing right in there with me. 

She tries to make it easier on us both by bringing over those pre-cooked dinners from the deli at the grocery store down on Elm Street. They remodeled that whole store last spring, you know. Put in these terrible, bright lights that just give you a headache if you look up, and they moved the bakery section completely around to the other side. I used to go in there every Wednesday morning just for Mr. Rossi's sourdough bread. He was the head baker there for thirty years... he had this wonderful, thick white mustache that curled right up at the ends, like something out of an old movie. His wife, Maria, she used to cross-stitch these little bluebirds onto the pockets of his aprons. She was so talented with a needle and thread. She actually won a blue ribbon at the county fair once for a quilt she made entirely out of old flour sacks... 

But, yes... my basic needs. I just can't take care of them on my own anymore. The flatness just makes it impossible.

He was always trying to fix things with duct tape. He taped up the handle on the lawnmower, the hinge on the screen door... he even tried to fix a leaky pipe under the sink once. He got it at the hardware store downtown, the one that closed when the big home improvement place opened out by the highway. It was a shame, really. They were those soft peppermints, the ones that just melt in your mouth, wrapped in clear plastic with little red stripes. I always kept a few in my pocketbook for Sunday service. Reverend Thomas would preach for over an hour sometimes, and you just needed a little something sweet to keep your throat from getting dry. Arthur used to polish it every Sunday afternoon with this special beeswax polish that smelled like lemons and old wood. He'd rub it in tiny little circles with an old flannel shirt he'd cut up into rags. He wouldn't let me use any of the good dust cloths on it, said they'd scratch the finish. He was so particular about his rags. He kept them in a coffee can in the garage—an old Maxwell House can, the blue one with the plastic lid. We used to drink Maxwell House every morning, brewed in this percolator that made the whole kitchen smell like heaven...\n\nBut the flatness... no, I haven't noticed it getting worse. It's just constant. Just... always exactly the same.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the flatness inside is affecting your daily life and ability to function?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it... it really is affecting everything. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... I suppose it is. I used to be so organized, you know. I'd plan all our meals for the week on Sunday afternoons and keep my calendar perfectly straight. Now, I just... I stare at a grocery list or the schedule for the senior center, and my mind just goes completely blank. I can't figure out what to do. My daughter has to help me decide even the most simple things now. 

She's been so good about it, my Sarah. She's a teacher, you know. Teaches second grade over at Lincoln Elementary. It's the same school she went to when she was a little girl. She had Mrs. Gable for second grade... oh, Mrs. Gable was a wonderful teacher. She used to play the piano for the children every morning. An old upright piano that sat right in the corner of the classroom. It was painted this awful shade of pea-green, but it sounded just beautiful. The keys were real ivory, not like the plastic they use today. My sister Margaret had an ivory necklace once. She bought it on a trip to India back in 1968. She went with her husband, Robert, for their twentieth anniversary. They rode on an elephant... can you imagine? Margaret was terrified of heights, but she said the elephant was so gentle. They brought back this beautiful silk sari for me, a deep sapphire blue with gold threads woven all through the border...

But yes, decisions... I just can't make them anymore. My mind just empties out when I try to think about what to do.

From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor... I came because... I just don't feel right anymore. Even when my children come over and sit with me on the porch, or when the sun is shining so beautifully on my garden—oh, you should see my hydrangeas this year, they finally bloomed after I moved them to the east side of the house. I always told my late husband they needed morning sun, but he insisted on planting them by the oak tree. He was stubborn like that, bless him. He bought that oak tree as a sapling back in '78... \n\nBut anyway, what I mean is, even when I'm looking at those flowers or sitting with my kids, I just feel completely flat inside. It's like the part of me that used to feel glad or... or comforted by those things just completely shut off. I don't know where the feeling went.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's been... I'd say about two weeks now. Yes, just about two weeks. It started right after my husband collapsed. He was right there in the kitchen, and I... I was making a pot roast. You know, the butcher down at the corner market—well, his son runs it mostly now, but he always saves the best chuck roasts for me because I've been shopping there since 1985. That warm, happy feeling I used to get when I finished a row of stitches or when the kids come to visit... it's just gone. It's like the switch for feeling glad about anything has just been completely turned off.\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel a sense of connection or pleasure when spending time with your children, or does that also feel flat to you?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, it just... it feels completely flat. Even with my children. They try to be so sweet, coming over and checking on me. My daughter Susan brought over a casserole just yesterday. She makes it with those little crispy fried onions on top, you know the ones that come in the can? I used to make it for Arthur every Thanksgiving. He wouldn't eat the green beans unless they were smothered in cream of mushroom soup and those onions. Thanksgiving was always such a big production in our house. We'd have twenty people sometimes, back when Arthur's brothers were still alive. We'd have to set up two card tables in the living room just to fit everyone, and the noise... oh, the noise was something else. \n\nBut... yes, to answer your question, when Susan brought that casserole over and sat with me, I just... I didn't feel anything. I knew I should feel grateful, or happy to see her, but it was just... nothing. Just flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Weight? Oh, I don't really know. I haven't stepped on a scale in... well, it's been ages. I used to have one of those nice mechanical ones, the kind with the little dial that spins around. Arthur bought it for me back in, oh, it must have been 1998. We got it at Sears. Do you remember when Sears used to be the place to go for everything? We bought our first refrigerator there, too. A big avocado green thing. It matched the stove perfectly. That was when everyone was doing their kitchens in avocado green or harvest gold. We had this terrible floral wallpaper to go with it... I finally made Arthur help me steam it off about ten years later, and what a mess that was. Little pieces of paper stuck to our shoes for weeks... \n\nBut, oh, my weight. No, I haven't noticed any change. I just haven't been paying attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your husband's collapse or anything else?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I... I don't think so. I mean, it happened so fast. One minute he was just sitting there in his favorite recliner—the brown leather one, you know? We got that chair at a discount furniture place out on Route 9. The man there, I think his name was Sal, he gave us such a good deal because there was a tiny scratch on the back leg. You couldn't even see it unless you got down on your hands and knees. Arthur loved that chair. He'd sit there every evening watching Jeopardy, shouting out the answers. He was always so good at history questions, but he couldn't get the pop culture ones to save his life. He didn't know who Madonna was until the late nineties... \n\nBut, no, doctor. I don't feel guilty. I just... I don't feel much of anything at all.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No... no, I don't think so. I was just looking out the window this morning, at my garden. The sun was hitting the hydrangeas just right. Arthur and I planted those hydrangeas when we first moved into the house. Oh, they must be forty years old now. We had to dig up this awful rocky soil to get them in, and Arthur broke his favorite trowel doing it. He was so mad about that trowel, he tried to fix it with duct tape, but it never held. He was always trying to fix things with duct tape. He taped up the handle on the lawnmower, the hinge on the screen door... he even tried to fix a leaky pipe under the sink once. Let me tell you, that was a disaster. We had water all over the linoleum, and we had to call a plumber anyway. The plumber, a nice young man named Ricky, charged us an arm and a leg, but he said Arthur's tape job was the funniest thing he'd ever seen...\n\nBut the garden... yes. The sun was on it, and it looked so pretty, but I just... I felt flat inside. Even when the kids sit with me, like Susan yesterday, it's the same. It's like the part of me that could feel glad about the flowers, or comforted by my children... it just shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself since your husband's collapse?\n\nPatient: Hurting myself? Oh, heavens, no. I haven't thought about anything like that. I wouldn't even know how to... well, I suppose people do all sorts of things. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable—she lived two houses down, the one with the big oak tree in the front yard that drops acorns all over the sidewalk every fall. Arthur used to spend hours sweeping those acorns up. He said if he didn't, someone would slip and break their neck, and then we'd be sued. He was always worrying about being sued. He bought this special push broom just for the acorns, with these stiff red bristles. He got it at the hardware store downtown, the one that closed when the big home improvement place opened out by the highway. It was a shame, really. The man who owned it, Mr. Henderson, he always gave the neighborhood kids a free piece of hard candy from a glass jar on the counter. Peppermint, usually, or butterscotch. Arthur loved butterscotch. He kept a roll of those little yellow candies in the glove compartment of the car, and they'd get all melted together in the summer...\n\nBut no, doctor. No thoughts like that. I'm just sitting here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience any pleasure at all, even in small amounts?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No, I... I don't think I can. I mean, I was just saying about the garden, wasn't I? The sun was shining on it, and it was so pretty, but I just felt... flat. Empty, really. And it's the same with my children. Susan came over yesterday. She brought over a casserole, one of those tuna noodle ones with the crushed potato chips on top. I used to make those for the kids when they were little. Arthur hated it, though. He always said tuna should be in a sandwich with mayonnaise and a piece of crisp lettuce, not baked in an oven. He was very particular about his sandwiches. He'd only eat that one brand of bread, the kind in the yellow wrapper with the red letters. If I bought the store brand, he'd know right away. He said the texture was all wrong, like eating a sponge. He could be so stubborn about food. Once, we went to this Italian restaurant for our anniversary, and he sent his spaghetti back three times because the sauce wasn't hot enough... \n\nBut, yes... even when Susan is sitting right there next to me, or when I'm looking at the flowers, it's just... nothing. It's like that part of me, the part that used to feel glad or comforted, it just completely shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in almost all activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Interest? Oh... yes, I suppose I have. I just don't have the energy for anything anymore. I used to knit, you know. I knitted all the time. Sweaters, scarves, little booties for the grandbabies when they came along. I had this beautiful basket next to my armchair, woven from willow branches. I bought it at a craft fair up in Vermont... oh, it must have been thirty years ago. Arthur and I took a trip up there to see the fall leaves. The colors were just spectacular that year, bright reds and oranges, like the whole mountain was on fire. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast that smelled like cinnamon and woodsmoke. The lady who ran it, Mrs. Higgins, she made the most wonderful blueberry pancakes. Arthur ate a whole stack of them by himself. He spilled syrup all down the front of his favorite flannel shirt—the green plaid one. I tried everything to get that stain out, soaking it, scrubbing it with that strong soap, but it always left a little shadow. He wore it anyway, right up until the elbows wore through... \n\nBut knitting... no, I haven't picked up my needles since... since it happened. I just look at the basket and I can't even imagine wanting to make anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience any pleasure at all, even in very small amounts or for a brief moment?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? I... I thought we just talked about this, doctor. But, no. No, I don't feel anything like that. Not even for a second. It's just... flat. Like I was saying about the garden, and Susan coming over. She's such a good girl, Susan. She works so hard at that real estate office. Always showing houses on the weekends. I don't know how she manages it with the two boys. Timmy, the older one, he just started playing baseball. Little League. He's a catcher. Arthur would have loved that. Arthur played baseball in high school, you know. He was a pitcher. He had this old leather mitt he kept in a box in the attic. Sometimes he'd get it out and rub it down with this special oil that smelled like pine needles. He said you had to take care of leather or it would dry out and crack. Just like skin, he'd say. He had very dry hands in the winter. He'd put on that thick white lotion before bed, the kind that comes in the blue jar, and he'd wear white cotton gloves to sleep so it wouldn't get all over the sheets. I used to tease him about looking like a magician in bed...\n\nBut no... to answer your question, doctor. No pleasure at all. Not even for a moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience any pleasure at all, even in things that you would normally find very small amounts of joy in?\n\nPatient: I... I feel like we're going in circles, doctor. I keep trying to explain it. I came here because... well, like I said, Susan sits right there with me, or I look out at the garden when the sun is shining so pretty on it, and there's just... nothing. I feel completely flat inside. It's exactly like that part of me, the part that could be glad or comforted by those things, just shut off entirely. \n\nIt reminds me of when the electricity goes out during a winter storm. We had this terrible ice storm back in '98. The power lines were down for a week. The house got so cold we had to sleep in our winter coats in the living room, huddled around the fireplace. Arthur kept feeding the fire with these big oak logs he'd chopped in the fall. He was so proud of that woodpile. He stacked it perfectly behind the shed, alternating the direction of the logs so it would breathe and dry out properly. He said if you don't stack it right, it rots from the inside out. He had this heavy axe with a hickory handle... he kept the blade so sharp you could shave with it, he used to joke. He'd spend hours out there on Saturday mornings, just chopping wood. The sound of it... thwack, thwack... it would echo all through the neighborhood...\n\nBut, yes. Flat inside. That's exactly how it is. No joy at all, not even a little bit.\n\nDoctor: How many days in a row have you been feeling this flatness inside?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been about two weeks now. Ever since Arthur... ever since he collapsed. Right there on the kitchen floor. The paramedics, they came rushing in through the back door... the screen door slammed so hard it knocked the little wooden sign off the wall. The one that says \"Bless this Mess.\" My sister gave me that sign when we first bought the house. She found it at a little antique shop in Cape Cod. We used to go to the Cape every summer when we were girls. We'd rent this tiny cottage near the beach, and the sand would get into absolutely everything. Our beds, our shoes, even the sandwiches my mother packed for lunch. She always made egg salad sandwiches on white bread, wrapped tight in wax paper. I can still taste the crunch of the sand mixed with the egg salad...\n\nBut yes, two weeks. Ever since they were working on him right there on the linoleum. It's been exactly two weeks of just... nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this flatness inside is something that you cannot escape, no matter what you are doing?\n\nPatient: Yes, I can't escape it at all. Like I keep saying, that's exactly why I came to see you in the first place. Even when my children sit right there with me, or the sun is shining so beautifully on my garden, I just feel completely flat inside. It's like the part of me that could be glad or comforted by any of it just shut off entirely. \n\nJust... click, and it's gone. Like a broken light switch. We had a broken switch in the hallway for the longest time, right by the coat closet. Arthur kept meaning to fix it. He bought the replacement part at Miller's Hardware downtown—the old one with the squeaky wooden floors. Mr. Miller used to give out peppermint candies from a big glass jar by the cash register. They were those soft peppermints, the ones that just melt in your mouth, wrapped in clear plastic with little red stripes. I always kept a few in my pocketbook for Sunday service. Reverend Thomas would preach for over an hour sometimes, and you just needed a little something sweet to keep your throat from getting dry. He had this booming voice that would just rattle the stained glass windows, especially when he got worked up about the Old Testament... \n\nBut, yes. I can't escape the flatness. No matter what I'm doing, that part of me just stays shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the flatness inside is getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: I don't... I don't know if it's getting worse. It's just... there. It's just always there, this heavy, empty feeling. It doesn't seem to get better or worse, it just stays the same. \n\nIt's like... it's like that old grandfather clock we have in the hallway. The one Arthur's father left him. It just ticks and ticks, the exact same rhythm, day in and day out. Tick, tock, tick, tock. Never faster, never slower. It's made of this beautiful dark mahogany. Arthur used to polish it every Sunday afternoon with this special beeswax polish that smelled like lemons and old wood. He'd rub it in tiny little circles with an old flannel shirt he'd cut up into rags. He wouldn't let me use any of the good dust cloths on it, said they'd scratch the finish. He was so particular about his rags. He kept them in a coffee can in the garage—an old Maxwell House can, the blue one with the plastic lid. We used to drink Maxwell House every morning, brewed in this percolator that made the whole kitchen smell like heaven...\n\nBut the flatness... no, I haven't noticed it getting worse. It's just constant. Just... always exactly the same.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the flatness inside is affecting your daily life and ability to function?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it... it really is affecting everything. I've completely stopped going down to the senior center. I used to go every Tuesday and Thursday for the knitting circle. We were all working on these little caps for the premature babies at the hospital. Helen always brought her famous lemon squares... oh, they were so tart they'd make your jaw ache, but you couldn't eat just one. She used the lemons right off the tree in her daughter's backyard in Florida. Her daughter moved down there... oh, it must be fifteen years ago now. Married a man who sold boats or something like that. They have a lovely house with a pool enclosed in one of those big screens to keep the mosquitoes out... \n\nBut I just can't bring myself to go anymore. I can't even manage the simple things here at home. Meals, running errands, keeping track of my calendar... my daughter has had to step in and do all of it for me. I mostly just stay in the house now. And I... I try to avoid the kitchen completely unless someone is in there with me. I just can't be in there alone. Not since Arthur...\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the flatness inside is making it hard for you to take care of your basic needs?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, that's... that's exactly what I was just trying to say a moment ago. I just can't do any of it. My daughter has to manage all my meals and run all my errands now because I just stay shut up in the house. I don't even look at my calendar anymore to know what day it is. And like I said, I won't even go into the kitchen to fix myself a simple piece of toast unless she's standing right in there with me. \n\nShe tries to make it easier on us both by bringing over those pre-cooked dinners from the deli at the grocery store down on Elm Street. They remodeled that whole store last spring, you know. Put in these terrible, bright lights that just give you a headache if you look up, and they moved the bakery section completely around to the other side. I used to go in there every Wednesday morning just for Mr. Rossi's sourdough bread. He was the head baker there for thirty years... he had this wonderful, thick white mustache that curled right up at the ends, like something out of an old movie. His wife, Maria, she used to cross-stitch these little bluebirds onto the pockets of his aprons. She was so talented with a needle and thread. She actually won a blue ribbon at the county fair once for a quilt she made entirely out of old flour sacks... \n\nBut, yes... my basic needs. I just can't take care of them on my own anymore. The flatness just makes it impossible.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the flatness inside is interfering with your ability to make decisions?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose it is. I used to be so organized, you know. I'd plan all our meals for the week on Sunday afternoons and keep my calendar perfectly straight. Now, I just... I stare at a grocery list or the schedule for the senior center, and my mind just goes completely blank. I can't figure out what to do. My daughter has to help me decide even the most simple things now. \n\nShe's been so good about it, my Sarah. She's a teacher, you know. Teaches second grade over at Lincoln Elementary. It's the same school she went to when she was a little girl. She had Mrs. Gable for second grade... oh, Mrs. Gable was a wonderful teacher. She used to play the piano for the children every morning. An old upright piano that sat right in the corner of the classroom. It was painted this awful shade of pea-green, but it sounded just beautiful. The keys were real ivory, not like the plastic they use today. My sister Margaret had an ivory necklace once. She bought it on a trip to India back in 1968. She went with her husband, Robert, for their twentieth anniversary. They rode on an elephant... can you imagine? Margaret was terrified of heights, but she said the elephant was so gentle. They brought back this beautiful silk sari for me, a deep sapphire blue with gold threads woven all through the border...\n\nBut yes, decisions... I just can't make them anymore. My mind just empties out when I try to think about what to do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, let me think... it's been about two weeks now. Yes, exactly two weeks since... since my husband collapsed. The paramedics were working on him right there in our kitchen. They had all their equipment spread out, and they pushed the kitchen table all the way up against the bay window to make room. 

That table is solid oak, you know. It's terribly heavy. We bought it at an estate sale over in Willow Creek... oh, it must have been thirty-five years ago. The woman running the sale was wearing the most peculiar hat. It was a big velvet thing, deep plum colored, with a real peacock feather sticking straight up out of the band. I remember it so clearly because Arthur just couldn't stop staring at it. Peacocks are such beautiful birds, but they are terribly noisy, aren't they? We went to the county zoo with the grandchildren once, and they had peacocks just roaming completely free on the walking paths. Little Timmy—that's my oldest grandson, he's a certified public accountant up in Chicago now, doing very well for himself—he was only four at the time. He dropped his ice cream cone right on the hot pavement because one of those birds squawked so loud it startled him. It was pistachio ice cream, too. His absolute favorite. He cried for twenty minutes until Arthur bought him another one... 

But yes, the flatness... it started right after that terrible day in the kitchen. About two weeks ago.

We'd have twenty people sometimes, back when Arthur's brothers were still alive. Do you remember when Sears used to be the place to go for everything? We bought our first refrigerator there, too. A big avocado green thing. It matched the stove perfectly. That was when everyone was doing their kitchens in avocado green or harvest gold. We had this terrible floral wallpaper to go with it... I finally made Arthur help me steam it off about ten years later, and what a mess that was. Little pieces of paper stuck to our shoes for weeks... \n\nBut, oh, my weight. No, I haven't noticed any change. I just haven't been paying attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your husband's collapse or anything else?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I... I don't think so. I mean, it happened so fast. One minute he was just sitting there in his favorite recliner—the brown leather one, you know? We got that chair at a discount furniture place out on Route 9. The man there, I think his name was Sal, he gave us such a good deal because there was a tiny scratch on the back leg. You couldn't even see it unless you got down on your hands and knees. Arthur loved that chair. He'd sit there every evening watching Jeopardy, shouting out the answers. He was always so good at history questions, but he couldn't get the pop culture ones to save his life. He didn't know who Madonna was until the late nineties... \n\nBut, no, doctor. I don't feel guilty. I just... I don't feel much of anything at all.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No... no, I don't think so. I was just looking out the window this morning, at my garden. The sun was hitting the hydrangeas just right. Arthur and I planted those hydrangeas when we first moved into the house. Oh, they must be forty years old now. We had to dig up this awful rocky soil to get them in, and Arthur broke his favorite trowel doing it. He was so mad about that trowel, he tried to fix it with duct tape, but it never held. He was always trying to fix things with duct tape. He taped up the handle on the lawnmower, the hinge on the screen door... he even tried to fix a leaky pipe under the sink once. Let me tell you, that was a disaster. We had water all over the linoleum, and we had to call a plumber anyway. The plumber, a nice young man named Ricky, charged us an arm and a leg, but he said Arthur's tape job was the funniest thing he'd ever seen...\n\nBut the garden... yes. Oh, heavens, no. I haven't thought about anything like that. I wouldn't even know how to... well, I suppose people do all sorts of things. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable—she lived two houses down, the one with the big oak tree in the front yard that drops acorns all over the sidewalk every fall. Arthur used to spend hours sweeping those acorns up. He said if he didn't, someone would slip and break their neck, and then we'd be sued. He was always worrying about being sued. He bought this special push broom just for the acorns, with these stiff red bristles. He got it at the hardware store downtown, the one that closed when the big home improvement place opened out by the highway. It was a shame, really. The man who owned it, Mr. Henderson, he always gave the neighborhood kids a free piece of hard candy from a glass jar on the counter. Peppermint, usually, or butterscotch. Arthur loved butterscotch. He kept a roll of those little yellow candies in the glove compartment of the car, and they'd get all melted together in the summer...\n\nBut no, doctor. No thoughts like that. Susan came over yesterday. She brought over a casserole, one of those tuna noodle ones with the crushed potato chips on top. I used to make those for the kids when they were little. Arthur hated it, though. He always said tuna should be in a sandwich with mayonnaise and a piece of crisp lettuce, not baked in an oven. He was very particular about his sandwiches. He'd only eat that one brand of bread, the kind in the yellow wrapper with the red letters. If I bought the store brand, he'd know right away. He said the texture was all wrong, like eating a sponge. He could be so stubborn about food. Once, we went to this Italian restaurant for our anniversary, and he sent his spaghetti back three times because the sauce wasn't hot enough... \n\nBut, yes... even when Susan is sitting right there next to me, or when I'm looking at the flowers, it's just... nothing. I bought it at a craft fair up in Vermont... oh, it must have been thirty years ago. Arthur and I took a trip up there to see the fall leaves. The colors were just spectacular that year, bright reds and oranges, like the whole mountain was on fire. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast that smelled like cinnamon and woodsmoke. He had this old leather mitt he kept in a box in the attic. Sometimes he'd get it out and rub it down with this special oil that smelled like pine needles. He said you had to take care of leather or it would dry out and crack. Just like skin, he'd say. He had very dry hands in the winter. He'd put on that thick white lotion before bed, the kind that comes in the blue jar, and he'd wear white cotton gloves to sleep so it wouldn't get all over the sheets. Not even for a moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience any pleasure at all, even in things that you would normally find very small amounts of joy in?\n\nPatient: I... I feel completely flat inside. It's exactly like that part of me, the part that could be glad or comforted by those things, just shut off entirely. \n\nIt reminds me of when the electricity goes out during a winter storm. We had this terrible ice storm back in '98. The power lines were down for a week. The house got so cold we had to sleep in our winter coats in the living room, huddled around the fireplace. Arthur kept feeding the fire with these big oak logs he'd chopped in the fall. He was so proud of that woodpile. He stacked it perfectly behind the shed, alternating the direction of the logs so it would breathe and dry out properly. He said if you don't stack it right, it rots from the inside out. He had this heavy axe with a hickory handle... he kept the blade so sharp you could shave with it, he used to joke. He'd spend hours out there on Saturday mornings, just chopping wood. The one that says \"Bless this Mess.\" My sister gave me that sign when we first bought the house. She found it at a little antique shop in Cape Cod. We used to go to the Cape every summer when we were girls. We'd rent this tiny cottage near the beach, and the sand would get into absolutely everything. Our beds, our shoes, even the sandwiches my mother packed for lunch. She always made egg salad sandwiches on white bread, wrapped tight in wax paper. They were those soft peppermints, the ones that just melt in your mouth, wrapped in clear plastic with little red stripes. I always kept a few in my pocketbook for Sunday service. Reverend Thomas would preach for over an hour sometimes, and you just needed a little something sweet to keep your throat from getting dry. No matter what I'm doing, that part of me just stays shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the flatness inside is getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: I don't... I don't know if it's getting worse. It's just... there. It's just always there, this heavy, empty feeling. Arthur used to polish it every Sunday afternoon with this special beeswax polish that smelled like lemons and old wood. He'd rub it in tiny little circles with an old flannel shirt he'd cut up into rags. He wouldn't let me use any of the good dust cloths on it, said they'd scratch the finish. Not since Arthur...\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the flatness inside is making it hard for you to take care of your basic needs?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, that's... that's exactly what I was just trying to say a moment ago. You couldn't even see it unless you got down on your hands and knees. Arthur loved that chair. He'd sit there every evening watching Jeopardy, shouting out the answers. He was always so good at history questions, but he couldn't get the pop culture ones to save his life. He didn't know who Madonna was until the late nineties... \n\nBut, no, doctor. He got it at the hardware store downtown, the one that closed when the big home improvement place opened out by the highway. It was a shame, really. He kept a roll of those little yellow candies in the glove compartment of the car, and they'd get all melted together in the summer...\n\nBut no, doctor. No thoughts like that. He'd only eat that one brand of bread, the kind in the yellow wrapper with the red letters. If I bought the store brand, he'd know right away. He said the texture was all wrong, like eating a sponge. The colors were just spectacular that year, bright reds and oranges, like the whole mountain was on fire. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast that smelled like cinnamon and woodsmoke. He had this heavy axe with a hickory handle... he kept the blade so sharp you could shave with it, he used to joke. He'd spend hours out there on Saturday mornings, just chopping wood. We used to go to the Cape every summer when we were girls. We'd rent this tiny cottage near the beach, and the sand would get into absolutely everything. Like I keep saying, that's exactly why I came to see you in the first place. It's like the part of me that could be glad or comforted by any of it just shut off entirely. \n\nJust... click, and it's gone. Like a broken light switch. We had a broken switch in the hallway for the longest time, right by the coat closet. Arthur kept meaning to fix it. He bought the replacement part at Miller's Hardware downtown—the old one with the squeaky wooden floors. Mr. Miller used to give out peppermint candies from a big glass jar by the cash register. They were those soft peppermints, the ones that just melt in your mouth, wrapped in clear plastic with little red stripes. I always kept a few in my pocketbook for Sunday service. Reverend Thomas would preach for over an hour sometimes, and you just needed a little something sweet to keep your throat from getting dry. He'd rub it in tiny little circles with an old flannel shirt he'd cut up into rags. He wouldn't let me use any of the good dust cloths on it, said they'd scratch the finish. He was so particular about his rags. He kept them in a coffee can in the garage—an old Maxwell House can, the blue one with the plastic lid. We used to drink Maxwell House every morning, brewed in this percolator that made the whole kitchen smell like heaven...\n\nBut the flatness... no, I haven't noticed it getting worse. It's just constant. Just... always exactly the same.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the flatness inside is affecting your daily life and ability to function?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it... it really is affecting everything. I've completely stopped going down to the senior center. I used to go every Tuesday and Thursday for the knitting circle. We were all working on these little caps for the premature babies at the hospital. Helen always brought her famous lemon squares... oh, they were so tart they'd make your jaw ache, but you couldn't eat just one. She used the lemons right off the tree in her daughter's backyard in Florida. Her daughter moved down there... oh, it must be fifteen years ago now. I mostly just stay in the house now. And I... I try to avoid the kitchen completely unless someone is in there with me. I just can't be in there alone. Not since Arthur...\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the flatness inside is making it hard for you to take care of your basic needs?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, that's... that's exactly what I was just trying to say a moment ago. I just can't do any of it. My daughter has to manage all my meals and run all my errands now because I just stay shut up in the house. I don't even look at my calendar anymore to know what day it is. And like I said, I won't even go into the kitchen to fix myself a simple piece of toast unless she's standing right in there with me. \n\nShe tries to make it easier on us both by bringing over those pre-cooked dinners from the deli at the grocery store down on Elm Street. They remodeled that whole store last spring, you know. Put in these terrible, bright lights that just give you a headache if you look up, and they moved the bakery section completely around to the other side. I used to go in there every Wednesday morning just for Mr. Rossi's sourdough bread. He was the head baker there for thirty years... he had this wonderful, thick white mustache that curled right up at the ends, like something out of an old movie. His wife, Maria, she used to cross-stitch these little bluebirds onto the pockets of his aprons. She was so talented with a needle and thread. She actually won a blue ribbon at the county fair once for a quilt she made entirely out of old flour sacks... \n\nBut, yes... my basic needs. I just can't take care of them on my own anymore. The flatness just makes it impossible.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the flatness inside is interfering with your ability to make decisions?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose it is. I used to be so organized, you know. I'd plan all our meals for the week on Sunday afternoons and keep my calendar perfectly straight. Now, I just... I stare at a grocery list or the schedule for the senior center, and my mind just goes completely blank. I can't figure out what to do. My daughter has to help me decide even the most simple things now. \n\nShe's been so good about it, my Sarah. She's a teacher, you know. Teaches second grade over at Lincoln Elementary. It's the same school she went to when she was a little girl. She had Mrs. Gable for second grade... oh, Mrs. Gable was a wonderful teacher. She used to play the piano for the children every morning. An old upright piano that sat right in the corner of the classroom. It was painted this awful shade of pea-green, but it sounded just beautiful. The keys were real ivory, not like the plastic they use today. My sister Margaret had an ivory necklace once. She bought it on a trip to India back in 1968. She went with her husband, Robert, for their twentieth anniversary. They rode on an elephant... can you imagine? Margaret was terrified of heights, but she said the elephant was so gentle. They brought back this beautiful silk sari for me, a deep sapphire blue with gold threads woven all through the border...\n\nBut yes, decisions... I just can't make them anymore. My mind just empties out when I try to think about what to do.\n\nDoctor: For how many days have you been feeling this flatness inside?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... it's been about two weeks now. Yes, exactly two weeks since... since my husband collapsed. The paramedics were working on him right there in our kitchen. They had all their equipment spread out, and they pushed the kitchen table all the way up against the bay window to make room. \n\nThat table is solid oak, you know. It's terribly heavy. We bought it at an estate sale over in Willow Creek... oh, it must have been thirty-five years ago. The woman running the sale was wearing the most peculiar hat. It was a big velvet thing, deep plum colored, with a real peacock feather sticking straight up out of the band. I remember it so clearly because Arthur just couldn't stop staring at it. Peacocks are such beautiful birds, but they are terribly noisy, aren't they? We went to the county zoo with the grandchildren once, and they had peacocks just roaming completely free on the walking paths. Little Timmy—that's my oldest grandson, he's a certified public accountant up in Chicago now, doing very well for himself—he was only four at the time. He dropped his ice cream cone right on the hot pavement because one of those birds squawked so loud it startled him. It was pistachio ice cream, too. His absolute favorite. Well, I... I really couldn't say if it's better or worse. The days just sort of bleed together now, you know? I don't even look at the clock in the hallway anymore to see how much time has passed. It's a beautiful old pendulum clock, solid cherry wood... Arthur's brother, Thomas, gave it to us for our wedding back in 1962. 

Thomas was a watchmaker, actually. He had a little shop right on Main Street, right next door to the old pharmacy that used to sell those wonderful malted milkshakes. They served them in those tall, frosted glasses with a paper straw. You just don't see paper straws like that anymore, do you? Well, I suppose they're bringing them back now for the environment, but the new ones get so terribly soggy. Thomas used to just eat his milkshakes with a spoon anyway. He said his front teeth were too sensitive for the cold. He had terrible teeth, poor Thomas. He finally got a full set of dentures when he turned sixty, and he was so proud of them he'd take them out to show people at dinner parties. Arthur would get so embarrassed, his face would turn completely red, right up to the tips of his ears... 

But, oh, what were you asking? Better or worse? I... I just don't know. I haven't noticed a difference. It all just feels exactly the same.

Arthur used to polish it every Sunday afternoon with this special beeswax polish that smelled like lemons and old wood. He'd rub it in tiny little circles with an old flannel shirt he'd cut up into rags. He wouldn't let me use any of the good dust cloths on it, said they'd scratch the finish. Put in these terrible, bright lights that just give you a headache if you look up, and they moved the bakery section completely around to the other side. I used to go in there every Wednesday morning just for Mr. Rossi's sourdough bread. He was the head baker there for thirty years... he had this wonderful, thick white mustache that curled right up at the ends, like something out of an old movie. His wife, Maria, she used to cross-stitch these little bluebirds onto the pockets of his aprons. She was so talented with a needle and thread. The flatness just makes it impossible.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the flatness inside is interfering with your ability to make decisions?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose it is. I used to be so organized, you know. I'd plan all our meals for the week on Sunday afternoons and keep my calendar perfectly straight. Now, I just... I stare at a grocery list or the schedule for the senior center, and my mind just goes completely blank. I can't figure out what to do. My daughter has to help me decide even the most simple things now. \n\nShe's been so good about it, my Sarah. She's a teacher, you know. Teaches second grade over at Lincoln Elementary. It's the same school she went to when she was a little girl. She had Mrs. Gable for second grade... oh, Mrs. Gable was a wonderful teacher. She used to play the piano for the children every morning. An old upright piano that sat right in the corner of the classroom. It was painted this awful shade of pea-green, but it sounded just beautiful. The keys were real ivory, not like the plastic they use today. My sister Margaret had an ivory necklace once. She bought it on a trip to India back in 1968. She went with her husband, Robert, for their twentieth anniversary. They rode on an elephant... can you imagine? Margaret was terrified of heights, but she said the elephant was so gentle. They brought back this beautiful silk sari for me, a deep sapphire blue with gold threads woven all through the border...\n\nBut yes, decisions... I just can't make them anymore. My mind just empties out when I try to think about what to do.\n\nDoctor: For how many days have you been feeling this flatness inside?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... it's been about two weeks now. Yes, exactly two weeks since... since my husband collapsed. The paramedics were working on him right there in our kitchen. They had all their equipment spread out, and they pushed the kitchen table all the way up against the bay window to make room. \n\nThat table is solid oak, you know. It's terribly heavy. We bought it at an estate sale over in Willow Creek... oh, it must have been thirty-five years ago. The woman running the sale was wearing the most peculiar hat. It was a big velvet thing, deep plum colored, with a real peacock feather sticking straight up out of the band. I remember it so clearly because Arthur just couldn't stop staring at it. Peacocks are such beautiful birds, but they are terribly noisy, aren't they? We went to the county zoo with the grandchildren once, and they had peacocks just roaming completely free on the walking paths. Little Timmy—that's my oldest grandson, he's a certified public accountant up in Chicago now, doing very well for himself—he was only four at the time. He dropped his ice cream cone right on the hot pavement because one of those birds squawked so loud it startled him. It was pistachio ice cream, too. His absolute favorite. He cried for twenty minutes until Arthur bought him another one... \n\nBut yes, the flatness... it started right after that terrible day in the kitchen. About two weeks ago.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the flatness inside is getting a little better or a little worse from day to day?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I really couldn't say if it's better or worse. The days just sort of bleed together now, you know? I don't even look at the clock in the hallway anymore to see how much time has passed. It's a beautiful old pendulum clock, solid cherry wood... Arthur's brother, Thomas, gave it to us for our wedding back in 1962. \n\nThomas was a watchmaker, actually. He had a little shop right on Main Street, right next door to the old pharmacy that used to sell those wonderful malted milkshakes. They served them in those tall, frosted glasses with a paper straw. You just don't see paper straws like that anymore, do you? Well, I suppose they're bringing them back now for the environment, but the new ones get so terribly soggy. He finally got a full set of dentures when he turned sixty, and he was so proud of them he'd take them out to show people at dinner parties. Arthur would get so embarrassed, his face would turn completely red, right up to the tips of his ears... \n\nBut, oh, what were you asking? Better or worse? I... I just don't know. I haven't noticed a difference. It all just feels exactly the same.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. He had a little shop right on Main Street, right next door to the old pharmacy that used to sell those wonderful malted milkshakes. They served them in those tall, frosted glasses with a paper straw. You just don't see paper straws like that anymore, do you? Well, I suppose they're bringing them back now for the environment, but the new ones get so terribly soggy. Thomas used to just eat his milkshakes with a spoon anyway. He said his front teeth were too sensitive for the cold. He had terrible teeth, poor Thomas. He finally got a full set of dentures when he turned sixty, and he was so proud of them he'd take them out to show people at dinner parties. Arthur would get so embarrassed, his face would turn completely red, right up to the tips of his ears... \n\nBut, oh, what were you asking? Better or worse? I... I just don't know. I haven't noticed a difference. It all just feels exactly the same."
